<meta>This page follows the symbolic frontispiece. It serves as the formal title page for the treatise, transitioning from the visual emblem of the Rosy Cross to a textual warning against the dangers of practical alchemy.</meta>

# A Letter
->to the<-

## Gold-hungry

# Lovers of Chemistry
->and<-
# Alchemy,

wherein they are well-meaningly advised, through various evidentiary reasons and refutations grounded in sound reason and experience <note>The author uses the word "Experience" to emphasize practical, empirical observation over mere theory.</note>, not to pursue this art <note>The "art" refers to the practice of <term>Chrysopoeia</term>, or the alchemical attempt to create gold.</note> any longer, so as not to plunge themselves into the utmost misery <note>This refers to the common historical occurrence of "gold-cooks" or amateur alchemists spending their entire fortunes on expensive materials and furnaces, only to end up in bankruptcy.</note>,

->by<-

**a true worshiper of truth,**
**and a sincere lover of his neighbor.**

Whoever knows the Abyssinian <note>Abyssinia is a historical name for Ethiopia. The "Abyssinian alphabet" refers to the Ge'ez script.</note> alphabet knows my name.

->Frankfurt and Leipzig.<-
->To be found in the Krauß Bookstore, 1770.<-

# Preface.

Not because common custom has made it almost a necessity to adorn every book (especially <term>chemical</term> <note>original: "Chymischen"; in the 17th and 18th centuries, alchemy and chemistry were often treated as a single discipline</note> ones) with a gilded preface and to send them out into the world—works which are filled more with flowery speeches, false promises, and supposedly true <term>secrets</term> <note>original: "Arcanis"; from the Latin 'arcanum,' referring to hidden knowledge or secret medicinal preparations</note>, or truths declared to be sincere and intended for the benefit of one's neighbor as their ultimate goal <note>original: "Endzweck"</note>; than with short-

Rather, my ultimate purpose is to
assure the reader that I wish to lead him on the path
of sound reason, and that I intend to advise him 
against those things toward which so
many false and dazzling 
promises might lead him. Since 
I share my true
opinion and way of thinking with him can-

unfounded prejudice—and hereby assure him

contained herein until

more all those souls deeply immersed in <term>alchemical</term> <note>original: "alchymistischen"</note> 
works to no longer

but rather to take to heart my warnings 
(which truly are not without foundation 
and <term>experience</term> <note>original: "Experience"; in this period, the term implies both personal experience and practical laboratory experimentation</note>). I

# Preface.

->A 3<-

I repeat once more, that no hatred has
taken hold of me. Urgent reasons have
moved me to share my thoughts with all
enthusiasts; but the primary
cause is the deplorable
downfall of so many honest and learned
men; love for my neighbor
obliges me to call them back from the path of
error; it is not that godless
neighborly love with which the <term>chemical writers</term> <note>original: "chymischen Scribenten"; a term used here for alchemical authors who published recipes and theories</note>
lull their admirers to sleep, and frequently
commit <term>blasphemy</term> <note>original: "Gotteslästerung"</note> by
beginning their <term>fraudulent processes</term> <note>original: "Lügenprocesse"; referring to alchemical recipes or laboratory procedures that the author considers deceptive</note>
in the name of the Most Holy <term>Trinity</term>,
and then concluding them with the oath,
"so help me God."
God is an enemy of lies.
Mine, however, are unshakeable truths, from
which one can derive more benefit than from
all other books. But
perhaps a blinded enthusiast will
cry out: "What! Who is the man
who presumes to deny everything?"
No one shall deny it to me: I have
seen this and that <term>coin</term> <note>original: "Münz"; refers to "alchemical coins" or medals supposedly struck from gold or silver created through transmutation to "prove" the art's success</note>,
(of which <term>Baron Schröter</term> <note>Likely Wilhelm von Schröter (1623–1688), a prominent writer who documented cases of alchemical transmutation</note>
makes a great fuss); they shout: "this person and that person ha—"

# Preface.

[...witnessed the metal] be <term>transmuted</term> <note>original: "tingirt"; referring to the alchemical process of *tincture*, where a substance is added to base metal to change its color and nature, supposedly into gold or silver</note>. Gently, gentlemen! It may
be that one or two such coins have been
seen; does it then immediately
follow that these were transformed
into the best gold or silver from lead, iron,
copper, or mercury by means of the supposed
<term>Universalissimum</term> <note>original: "Universalissimum"; a Latin term for the "Universal Medicine" or the Philosopher's Stone, the ultimate goal of alchemy believed to have universal powers of transformation</note>?
Oh, what deceptive phantoms lurk
beneath a shining mask; a well-
read enthusiast will surely know
that two kinds of <term>tinctures</term> <note>original: "Tincturen"; substances used to perform transmutation</note> are established:
one for the white [silver], and one for the
red [gold]. Has anyone, with his own eyes,
ever seen even a trial of the white
tincture? No, certainly not; everything
consists of hearsay, or from those
writings filled with lies. I
myself have been assured by people
that they saw <term>transmutation</term> <note>original: "tingiren"</note> with their own eyes;
but after I became more closely
acquainted with them, I discovered that
they followed this science so
deeply and blindly that it then
did not surprise me at all when, blinded by
their imagination—or better to say, their
phantoms—they believed they had
seen something. O God! Not everything that

I had a father and friends who wandered through half the world (to indulge this passion) and who had half the libraries of <term>chemical</term> <note>original: "chymischer"; at this time, chemistry and alchemy were often synonymous</note> writers in their heads; who, in their own right, were quite learned men, lacking neither in good laboratory skill, opportunity, nor wealth—yes! They even held in their hands <term>manuscripts</term> <note>original: "MS."</note> inherited from so-called <term>adepts</term> <note>original: "adeptis"; individuals believed to have attained the ultimate secrets of alchemy, specifically the Philosopher's Stone</note>, and yet they consumed their entire fortune without finding any other <term>stone</term> <note>The author is making a pun: they didn't find the Philosopher's Stone, only the "stone" that serves as a grave marker</note> besides the one that covered their lifeless bodies. Nothing is more absurd than when <term>chemical authors</term> <note>alchemical writers</note> try to force this art upon Adam, the patriarchs, or even King Solomon <note>original: "Salomoni Regi"</note> himself. How little of what is genuine do we have from the <term>antiquity</term> of bygone ages? And the little we do have, they do not understand; if they read the Scriptures better in their context, they would find nothing concerning the former <note>Adam and the patriarchs</note>, but for the latter <note>Solomon</note> it is quite clear from whence Solomon obtained his <term>wealth</term>. It might still be plausible if they [attributed it] to <term>Tubal-Cain</term> <note>A biblical figure from Genesis 4:22, described as an "instructor of every artificer in brass and iron," often claimed by alchemists as the first metalworker</note> ra-

# Preface.

by reason <note>original: "ratione"</note> of the discovery and working of contemptible iron, they had attributed this art to him. I do not wish to indulge in any further long-windedness here; I only ask once more that every enthusiast consider with pure reason the contents of these few pages, and let this warning serve for his benefit: set aside your coals and instruments <note>The author is telling alchemists to stop their expensive, practical experiments and think theoretically first</note>, grasp my *system*, and within this narrow space, this irrefutable *argument*. Like me, believe nothing so long as you are not convinced otherwise by reason and nature; yet *esteem* <note>original: "ästimire"</note> this science insofar as it *extends* <note>original: "extendirt"</note> to the benefit of the fatherland, and only for as long as your eye and reason do not convince you of something else. For the eye without reason is very deceptive; but if you are convinced of something else, I allow you to throw these pages into the fire and to consider me an *idiot* <note>original: "Idioten"; here meaning an unlearned or ignorant person</note>. I do not doubt that a great many will be found who will cover this little book with learned *physical controversies* <note>scientific disputes</note>,

# Preface.

I will gladly allow them to dip their learned pen into the vitriol water <note>original: "Vitriolwasser"; vitriol (sulfuric acid) here implies bitter or caustic writing</note>, to write everything imaginable against me, and to send such things under seal to the publisher of these pages, but with the sole condition <note>original: "Condition"</note> that they first examine themselves to see whether they are true Brothers of the Rosy Cross <note>original: "fratres Roseae Crucis"; members of a legendary secret society of mystics and alchemists</note>, Adepts <note>original: "Adepti"; those believed to have mastered the secrets of alchemy</note>, and sons <note>original: "filii"</note> of the fourth and fifth class. However, I would almost claim with certainty that no one will practically convince me otherwise or overturn my arguments <note>original: "Argumenta"</note>. The reader will thus find more light and truth in this small space than in many hundreds of other alchemical authors <note>original: "chymischen Autoribus"</note> who have written so much either for money, or to fool the world, or to hatch their mental fantasies <note>original: "Hirn-Insecten," literally "brain-insects," referring to eccentric whims or delusional ideas</note>. I see, moreover, in advance that many are nonetheless so absorbed, and rush with the greatest desire toward the pit <note>referring either to a literal mine or the metaphorical "pit" of financial and spiritual ruin</note> to plunge themselves into the greatest misfortune; well then, so be it, let them work on, yet I recommend <note>original: "recommendire"</note> metallurgy <note>original: "Metallurgie"</note> to them; through it, they may somewhat quiet their passion <note>original: "Passion"</note> and can create benefit for the fatherland, [be-] <meta>catchword: mü:; likely "bemühen" (strive/endeavor)</meta>

A 5

<summary>The author challenges his critics to prove their status as true alchemical masters before attacking his work, while warning that most alchemical literature is deceptive. He suggests that those obsessed with the craft should focus on practical metallurgy to benefit society rather than wasting themselves on false alchemy.</summary>
<keywords>Rosicrucians, Adepts, alchemical authors, metallurgy, mental fantasies, vitriol, fatherland, academic dispute</keywords>

<meta>This page concludes the preface, transitioning from the author's critique of fraudulent alchemists to a final piece of advice for the reader: to focus on the tangible, concentrated secrets of the physical world rather than losing oneself in abstract speculation.</meta>

# Preface.

...they should strive to discover the infinitely many hidden things still in this kingdom <note>referring to the mineral kingdom or the realm of nature</note>; there they will perhaps succeed sooner than in the discovery of the unknown islands in the philosophical sea <note>a metaphor for the vast and often confusing body of alchemical theory and literature</note>. Let them leave the upper realms in their immeasurable expanse <note>original: "Expanso"; a term used to describe the vastness of the heavens or the spiritual world</note> and stick to the lower, more concentrated matters <note>original: "Concentrirtere"; referring to the dense, physical reality of minerals and the earth</note>. I am,

### Highly Favored Reader!

->your sincere servant and friend<-

->the author.<-

## Letter to a Lover of Alchemy, in which he is well-meaningly advised against pursuing this study <term>theoretically and practically</term> <note>original: "theoretice & practice"; referring to both the reading of alchemical texts and the actual laboratory work</note> any longer.

### Dearest Friend!

With a secure opportunity, I herewith return in a cubic crate <note>a "Cubischen Verschlag" refers to a sturdy wooden packing case used for transporting heavy items like books</note> the chemical books lent to me, living in the hope that you will spare me from reading such books in the future; for otherwise, I would have to consider you my greatest enemy, since they seek to rob me of the precious time that I otherwise tend to employ for the benefit of the public <note>original: "Publici"; meaning the common good or the community at large</note>. Why do you wish to [force] such [things] upon me by force

<meta>catchword: upon-</meta>

<keywords>letter, chemical books, alchemical study, time management, public benefit, theoretical vs practical alchemy</keywords>

[I] will do it, not only to satisfy you, but also to improve you, and to heal you from your illness <note>The author views the recipient's obsession with alchemy as a spiritual or mental "sickness" to be cured through reason</note>. To reach my final objective, I will refute a book, which you specifically recommended to me, word for word, point by point <term>point by point</term> <note>original Latin: "punctatim"; meaning in every detail</note>. It bears the title: **Various Notations and Information for a Beginner and Lover of Chemical Science**; I therefore repeat the author's own contents.

First <note>original Latin: "Primo"</note>, he writes:
> I suppose <note>original: "Supponire ich"</note> that a lover of this noble as well as highly pleasant science will not believe that this art is like a trade, in which one proceeds merely mechanically <term>mechanically</term> <note>original Latin: "mechanice"; referring to rote physical labor or manual skill without theoretical understanding</note>, to melt the lead <note>original: "Bley"</note> and to heat the iron to a glow, and to forge it like a blacksmith on the anvil and beat it apart—no! But rather I hope that he is a true lover of the sciences, especially of philosophy <term>philosophy</term> <note>original: "Weltweisheit"; literally "world-wisdom," the standard 18th-century German term for philosophy or secular learning</note>, and in the unfathomable book of nature <note>A common early modern metaphor describing the natural world as a text written by God that must be decoded and read</note>, an untiring researcher <note>original: "Nachforscher"</note> and a lover devoted to study <note>original: "Studio"</note>.

Second <note>original Latin: "Secundo"</note>. When one feels this drive within oneself, to [achieve] something through one's reason...

<meta>This page continues the author's critique of a specific alchemical manuscript. On the previous page, the author introduced the text he is refuting—a guide for a "beginner and lover of chemical science"—and began discussing the difference between a mere manual trade and the deeper study of nature.</meta>

->⚜ ( A ) ⚜<-

to understand [them], he should apply himself to investigating exactly the natural first principles <term>first principles</term> <note>original: "Anfangsgründe"; the basic underlying elements or axioms of a subject</note>, the causes of every moving being, and the origin of individuals <note>original Latin: "individuorum"; in a philosophical context, this refers to specific, indivisible things or substances</note>.

3. This science is named a divine science for the reason that it teaches [one] to recognize nature (as the most noble thing after God), and from its active power and constantly moving essence, the knowledge of the Creator Himself may be demonstrated. Therefore, those err greatly who believe they can treat this science mechanically <term>mechanically</term> <note>original Latin: "mechanice"; referring to the practice of alchemy as a mere manual craft or rote procedure without spiritual or theoretical depth</note>.

4. Now, after this introduction, I wish to ask what my seeker <note>original: "Liebhaber"; literally "lover," a common term for a dedicated amateur or student of a "noble" art</note> searches for out of or through this knowledge? He will answer me: exactly that which all seekers would like to have, namely to be able to make gold and silver. Although this was not the noble longing of the philosophers <term>philosophers</term> <note>original: "Weltweiſen"; literally "world-wise ones," referring to those who seek deeper truths rather than material wealth</note>, I know well enough that the majority of mankind carries a desire only to be rich, regardless of the fact that this wild and vain impulse has deprived many of all they possessed. My seekers! This objective is thus not the be— <note>The text cuts off here at the catchword "ſte," forming the word "beſte" (best) on the following page</note>

<summary>The author argues that true science is a "divine" pursuit of understanding God through nature, rather than a "mechanical" pursuit of wealth. He critiques the common alchemist's motivation—the desire to manufacture gold and silver—as a "wild and vain impulse."</summary>
<keywords>divine science, first principles, individual substances, Creator, mechanics vs. philosophy, alchemy, greed, gold and silver</keywords>

The opening word "ste" completes a superlative adjective from the previous sentence—likely "edelste" (most noble)—describing the "divine science" or "knowledge of the Creator." ->* ( A ) *<-

[noble]st and most pleasing to the Deity, because the use of it (specifically <note>original Latin: "in specie"; specifically or particularly</note> in today’s world) is more of a hindrance than a benefit to man's true happiness.

5. But here is the main question (as we wish to continue on the subject of the Art <term>Art</term> <note>original: "Kunst"; in this context, the "Hermetic Art" or Alchemy</note>): by what means can this desired gold and silver be obtained? Is it not through the <term>Philosopher’s Stone</term> <note>original: "Stein der Weisen"; the legendary substance believed to facilitate the transmutation of base metals into gold and the creation of an elixir of life</note>! Yes, this—this is supposed to be it, oh God! And yet, this stone is the reef upon which so many thousands of people have suffered shipwreck; but no one pays heed to this, and even if a thousand have perished at sea, two thousand more venture out upon it. Very well then;

6. So let every seeker <note>original: "Liebhaber"; literally "lover," used here to describe a dedicated amateur or student of alchemy</note> examine himself quite closely. Is it not true that he wishes to learn how to prepare the <term>Philosopher’s Stone</term>? How? When? And from what source? He will be uncertain as to what kind of matter <term>matter</term> <note>original: "Materie"; the "Materia Prima" or starting physical substance required for the Great Work</note> he should use for it; yet he must have a starting material. Well then, I refer him (before he undertakes even the slightest manual work with his own hands) to the best authors <note>original Latin: "Authores"; the classical and medieval authorities whose texts were considered the essential foundation for any alchemical practitioner</note> as the master teachers of this...

11. The Matter is described by the Philosophers in so many different ways that one cannot easily get to the bottom of it without very great **study** <note>original: "Studio"</note> and divine **grace**; for divine grace leads us to understand the **authors**, regarding what they intend by a thing (which is indeed only one single thing) that lies hidden under so many various names; although it should be well **considered** that the Matter from which the Stone is made is not so difficult to discover as the rest—namely, what one must then do with it to bring the Work to its end. For the Matter has certain properties from which it can be recognized; but it is also quite certain that this Matter has a similarity to many others, and precisely because of this similarity, so many seekers <note>original: "Liebhaber"; literally "lovers" of the art, referring to aspiring alchemists.</note> are led astray, so that they fall now upon this, now upon that mineral or metal; I **exclude** <note>original: "präscendire"; from the Latin 'praescindere', meaning to set aside or disregard.</note> those who work entirely outside of and against Nature.

Sophists, who do not know how to form a logical conclusion. I advise them to form a proper <term>syllogism</term> <note>A formal logical argument consisting of a major premise, a minor premise, and a conclusion.</note>, regulating the major and minor premises <note>original: "majorem & minorem"</note> philosophically and according to nature; in this way, they will see from the consequence that they cannot fail.

13. In this manner, they must proceed in their theory before even laying a hand on the work, and so they will not ruin themselves in the least or cause damage to their income. By this means, those people who wish to deceive a seeker <note>original: "Liebhaber"; a "lover" or devoted student of alchemy.</note> and rob them of their money will find no audience. For how one should further guard against such people and their sophistical processes, one need only read the <term>Count of Trevisan</term> <note>original: "Comitem Trevisanum"; referring to Bernard of Trevisan, a famous 15th-century alchemist known for his warnings against fraudulent methods.</note> and the famous <term>Kunckel</term> <note>Johann von Löwenstern-Kunckel (1630–1703), a renowned chemist and alchemist who was often skeptical of "sophistical" or false alchemical claims.</note>.

14. No! Rather, as was said, one should seek it in good old authors of whom one is certain that they were true owners of this jewel <note>original: "Kleinods"; often used metaphorically to describe the Philosopher's Stone.</note>.

15. Now, however, it is necessary above all to know which of these is the <term>most universal</term> <note>original: "universalissimum"; referring to the highest medicine that can treat all diseases and all metals.</note>, and which, <term>with respect to</term> <note>original: "respectu"</note> this <term>most universal</term> substance, have been the particular tinctures; for between the <term>Elixir</term> <note>A potent alchemical liquid intended for transformation or healing.</note>, the <term>Iris</term> <note>Likely referring to the iridescent "peacock's tail" stage of the process, or a specific lower-level tincture named for its colors.</note>, and the <term>Stone</term> <note>original: "lapide"; the Philosopher's Stone, the ultimate solid goal of the Great Work.</note>, there is in fact an <term>essential</term> difference. One must know that many errors spring from this source, and that a seeker <note>original: "Liebhaber"; literally a "lover" of the art.</note> cannot easily become wise by reading the philosophical books if he does not grasp this difference. For here, the philosophers do not agree <term>in choosing the material</term> <note>original: "in eligenda materia"</note>, although they are in agreement regarding the <term>three principles</term> <note>original: "tribus principiis"; the triad of Sulfur, Mercury, and Salt from which all matter was thought to be composed.</note>. Even though their works passed through all the colors, even unto the Redness <note>The "Rubedo" or final red stage of the alchemical process.</note>, a true researcher and <term>hermetic son</term> <note>original: "filius hermeticus"; a dedicated initiate following the teachings of Hermes Trismegistus.</note> must nevertheless [know] the difference of the one from the [other]...

<meta>This page continues the discussion from page 24 regarding the generation of metals and why, despite having a common origin, most substances in nature do not naturally reach the perfection of gold without the intervention of the alchemist.</meta>

[...and why] they would not all become gold? Since indeed one, just like the other, was created from a single root and possesses the same seed, [one must ask] what gold itself lacks, so that it has been left to the artist to expand it and bring it to its <term>Quintessence</term> <note>The "fifth essence"; the purest and most concentrated form of a substance, beyond the four elements.</note>. He must learn to recognize exactly the properties of all <term>three kingdoms</term> <note>The Animal, Vegetable, and Mineral kingdoms. Alchemists believed a single life force or "seed" operated across all three, though it was most "fixed" in minerals.</note>, how they exist, what attraction and repulsion each possesses, what kind of connection they harbor toward one another, what is <term>homogeneous</term> and <term>heterogeneous</term> <note>original: "homogen und heterogen"; referring to substances that are either of the same nature and can be combined, or are of a different nature and thus repel or "kill" one another.</note> to them, and so forth—how one kingdom is to be united with another.

18. A <term>seeker</term> <note>original: "Liebhaber"; literally a "lover" of the art, referring to an aspiring alchemist or amateur researcher.</note> must consider that he has a <term>philosophical work</term> (a work grounded in nature) before him—a work which is not <term>purely speculative</term> <note>original: "pure speculativum"; referring to theoretical study alone.</note>, but <term>speculatively practical</term> <note>original: "speculative practicum"; a blend of theory and laboratory practice.</note>—and he should only observe the philosophical rules that have their foundation [in nature]. For according to the labor itself, it is material or mechanical; therefore, the <term>Adepts</term> <note>original: "Adepti"; those who have mastered the "Great Work" of creating the Philosopher's Stone.</note> also called it <term>Woman’s Work</term> <note>original: "Weiber-Werk"; a common alchemical term for the later, more passive stages of the process, such as gentle heating or washing, which require patience rather than physical strength or complex theory.</note>, though this is certainly not to be understood as applying to the beginning of this work.

...will instruct according to knowledge. The philosophers indeed speak of a chaos, of the separation of the elements, of the remote and proximate matter <note>original: "Materia remota et proxima"; 'remote matter' refers to the primal substance of all things, while 'proximate matter' refers to the substance closer to its final form in the alchemical process.</note>; all of this is good to know and is in itself the foundation of the whole Art <note>The "Art" refers to Alchemy or the "Great Work."</note>. Nevertheless, he must not seek such a thing too high, nor too low. That is: he must not strive to make his stone <note>original: "lapidem"; referring to the Philosopher's Stone.</note> directly from the elements, or from dew, rain, and snow, or from herbs, roots, eggs, meat, blood, bone, or urine. 

Much less should he seek it from "dead bodies" <note>In alchemy, "dead bodies" often referred to metals that had been processed or "killed" by common chemical methods, or literally organic remains.</note>, from whose metals—neither with nor through their conjunction <note>original: "Conjunction"; the chemical or symbolic marriage of two substances.</note>—can gold and silver be made, even if one learns to make beautiful tombac <note>original: "Tomback"; a brass alloy with a high copper content used to simulate the appearance of gold.</note> and white compositions; for this reason, it is still not gold and silver. 

But one must investigate from what metals and minerals were generated, in what their substance and essence actually consist, what their active and passive principles <note>original: "agens und patiens"; the 'acting' force (often associated with sulfur or heat) and the 'receiving' or 'suffering' matter (often associated with mercury or moisture).</note> might be, what they have too much or too little of regarding this or that property, what binds and holds them together, and through what they can be quite gently dissolved; what the cause is that one is more stable in the fire than the other, and why they [are created] by nature...

19. Everything that is comprehensible in this world has, as has already been said, been divided by the Creator of all things into three kingdoms, namely into the Animal, Vegetable, and Mineral. Now, **in proportion** <note>original: "à proportione"</note> to these three kingdoms, one must also seek **according to the nature of the Kingdoms** <note>original: "secundum naturam Regnorum"</note> only that which is to be found within them according to their potential. Even though there is a **universal** <note>original: "universale"; a single primary substance or force believed by alchemists to permeate all three realms of existence.</note> which is found in all three, if one wants to have gold, one must take it from no other than the mineral kingdom, and seek it in that very place from which gold and all metals and minerals were created. The Sages name their seed and say that it has sprung from nothing else but **from Sulfur and Mercury** <note>original: "ex Sulphure et Mercurio"; refers to the alchemical principles of "Sulfur" (the soul/combustible principle) and "Mercury" (the spirit/fluid principle), rather than the common chemical elements.</note>, but—let it be well understood—not from the common kind. And **Johannes Scotus** <note>Johannes Scotus Eriugena (c. 815–877), a Neoplatonist philosopher whose views on the division of nature were influential in later hermetic thought.</note> says: the first matter of all metals is formed **from coagulable quicksilver** <note>original: "ex argento vivo coagulabili"</note> and **sulfurous quicksilver** <note>original: "argento vivo sulphureo"</note>. If one were to take this **literally** <note>original: "secundum litteram"</note>, he might well have a hundred years of work to do before he could produce a metal from raw sulfur and raw **Mercury** <term>Quicksilver</term> <note>original: "Mercurio"; the liquid metal mercury, used here as a symbol for the volatile principle in the alchemical process.</note> that would richly repay his effort. But when one knows...

20. Why, however, it has found so few devotees until now, **specifically** <note>original: "in specie"</note> great lords, is not at all surprising when one looks closely at how they go about it. <meta>This page continues the discussion on the preparation of the Philosopher's Stone, specifically focusing on the concept of "reduction" and the correct philosophical way to dissolve matter, contrasting the true alchemist with the mere goldsmith or "sophist."</meta>

<page-num>34</page-num>

<header>->❦ ( ⁂ ) ❦<-</header>

[...which] is so common that every peasant has it; for the *philosophers* <note>original: "Philosophi"; the master alchemists of old.</note> say that outside of this *reduction* <note>original: "Reduction"; the process of returning a substance to its primary, simple state or "first matter."</note>, (which every devotee <note>original: "Liebhaber"; a student or seeker of the Great Work.</note> must observe as a fundamental rule <note>original: "pro fundamentali regula"</note>,) the Philosopher's Stone <note>original: "Stein der Weisen"</note> cannot be prepared.

26. How many already fail in this first *reduction* cannot be sufficiently described; it is one thing to *solvate* <note>original: "solviren"; to dissolve a solid into a liquid state.</note>, another to *resolve* <note>original: "resolviren"; to break a substance down into its constituent elements.</note>, another to work like a *philosopher* <note>original: "Philosophus"</note>, and quite another to work like a *goldsmith* <note>original: "Goldschmid"</note>. Whence come these errors? Only from ignorance and from sophistic bungling; for tearing apart with "sharp waters" <note>likely referring to strong mineral acids like aqua fortis or aqua regia.</note> is not called dissolving, much less *calcining* <note>original: "calciniren"; the process of heating a substance to high temperatures to effect oxidation or change.</note>; for the one *corrodes* and is an enemy, but the true *solvent* <note>original: "Solvens"</note> only softens, and is a friend and a *homogeneous* <note>meaning of the same nature or kind.</note> being of the *solute* <note>original: "Soluti"; the substance being dissolved.</note>.

27. 33. Furthermore, the **Philosophers** <note>In alchemical texts, "Philosophers" refers to the respected sages and experimentalists of the past, such as Hermes Trismegistus or Geber.</note> speak in their **parabolic** <note>Symbolic or allegorical stories intended to veil secret knowledge from the uninitiated.</note> writings of the **Man** and **Woman**; namely, how the **Woman** must first have the upper hand over the **Man**, and afterwards the **Man** would gain the upper hand, etc. Anyone who is even slightly well-read will know that **students of nature** <note>original: "Naturkündiger," referring to natural philosophers or early scientists.</note> predominately assign **moisture** and **cold** to the female sex, but **heat** and **dryness** to the male. Now, if I take a substance in which the first two qualities predominate, and allow the moisture to dry out gradually by a gentle fire, the more it dries, the hotter it becomes; thus it becomes true that the **Man** overcomes the **Woman**. This is **by reason** <note>original Latin: "ex ratione"</note> of the fact that the **heat** was already hidden in the **matter** <note>original: "Materie"</note> from the very beginning, and was only surrounded by superfluous moisture (**let the superfluous be removed**) <note>original Latin: "amoveatur superfluum"</note>. For as soon as the **hidden mover** <note>The "internal fire" or active principle within a substance that drives its chemical transformation.</note> receives a little help, it begins to claim dominion and gives no rest until it has it entirely in its possession. From this, the **lover** <note>original: "Liebhaber," here meaning a "Lover of the Art," a common term for a devoted student of alchemy.</note> sees once again how the philosophical **allegories** are meant to be understood.

34. And once one begins to understand such things, and pays attention to their intended meaning rather than their literal wording, one should surely be able to guess what their **Philosophic Matter** <note>original: "Materia Philosophica," the primary raw substance used to begin the Great Work of alchemy.</note> might be, and what requirements it must possess to produce that which is sought from it (for where there is nothing, nothing can be found). This should be understood in **quantity** and **quality**. What, then, is their so-often-mentioned **Mercury** <note>original: "Mercurius," not necessarily the liquid metal we know today, but the fluid, transformative principle of all things.</note>? What is their **philosophic fire**? To recognize the latter within the former, **this is the labor, this is the task** <note>original Latin: "hic labor hoc opus." A famous phrase from Virgil’s Aeneid, often used by alchemists to describe the difficulty of the work.</note>. What are their **Mountain**, their **Sea**, and their **water of life** <note>original Latin: "aqua vitæ," often referring to a powerful solvent or distilled spirit.</note>? What are their **Diana** <note>In the symbolic language of alchemy, Diana usually represents the Moon or Silver, or a state of pure, white crystallization.</note> and other such things supposed to be, which exist within and arise from their work? Many of these terms may perhaps be synonyms, while many other names signify the various changes or **stages** <note>original: "gradus"</note> of their work.

35. Although the **Adepts** <note>Those who have mastered the secrets of alchemy and successfully produced the Philosopher's Stone.</note> have hidden their **Mercury** under so many different forms, one will observe that they nevertheless wish it to be known that they have taken the true one from the **mineral kingdom**. However, one must also know at the same time that it is no metal, but a water—and indeed a **dry water** <note>original: "trockenes Wasser." This is a classic alchemical paradox describing a substance that flows like a liquid but does not wet the hands, often referring to metallic vapors or certain salts.</note>.

<meta>This page continues the discussion from page 42, which ended by describing the "fountain of Bernard" (the famous alchemical allegory of Bernard of Trevisan). The text here picks up mid-sentence, describing how the truth is often obscured in alchemical writings.</meta>

<page-num>43</page-num>
<header>⚜ ( A ) ⚜</header>

...something escaped there; but whoever believes they will find such things in a single **context** <note>original: "Contextu"; meaning a continuous, straightforward explanation or a single book.</note> is greatly deceived. Therefore, one must often read over a matter repeatedly, gather the scattered pieces together, and draw conclusions measured according to the order of nature. In this way, one will unfailingly guess their meanings and learn to understand their riddles—except, as has already been said, for the weight and their fire. These two they have unquestionably concealed and left behind a very poor account of them, as the **Count of the Mark** <note>original: "Graf von der Mark," referring to Bernard of Trevisan (1406–1490), a celebrated alchemist whose works emphasize the difficulty of finding the correct "fire."</note> himself testifies. Except for two or three authors, it is unlikely that anything is to be found, for they had many kinds of fire: the hidden fire of their **matter** <term>matter</term> <note>original: "Materie," the primary substance used in the Great Work.</note>, or the moving fire, the vaporous fire, the holy fire, **Vulcan's workshop** <note>A symbolic name for the intense heat of the furnace or the external heat applied by the chemist, named after the Roman god of fire and metalworking.</note>, and the like.

37. I must warn every seeker <note>original: "Liebhaber," literally "lover," a standard term for a dedicated student of the alchemical arts.</note> of the **Hermetic Art** <note>The tradition of alchemy and esoteric philosophy named after the mythical Hermes Trismegistus.</note> that he should take care to undertake nothing in coal-fire <note>The literal heat of burning charcoal or a common forge.</note>, raw works, in the melting of metals, or their **conjunctions** <term>conjunctions</term> <note>original: "Conjunctionen," the chemical or symbolic joining of two substances to form a new whole.</note>. This fire destroys everything, and the metals are **dead bodies** <note>Alchemists often considered processed metals to be "dead" because their "living spirit" had been fixed or removed; they sought instead a "living" metallic seed.</note>; therefore, one should simply reject [them] without hesi-

<summary>The author explains that alchemical secrets are intentionally scattered across different texts and warns that the most hidden secrets are the correct proportions (weight) and the nature of the fire. Readers are specifically warned against using literal "coal fire" or common metallurgical techniques, as these are "dead" processes.</summary>

Much could be said of this, as many ores would yield a far greater profit if this roasting did not occur. Whoever considers *antimony* to be the "philosophical lead" <note>a metaphorical primary material in alchemy, not to be confused with common lead</note> is greatly deceived. ...matter, as the <term>Philosophers</term> <note>original: "Philosophi"; referring to alchemists who sought the deeper secrets of nature.</note> have called it, they surely would have omitted it; however, under the names of <term>Saturn</term> <note>The alchemical name for lead.</note>, <term>Mars</term> <note>The alchemical name for iron.</note>, and <term>Antimony</term>, they understood an entirely different essence than the <term>Matter</term> or <term>Mineral</term> <note>original: "Materia oder Minera"</note> as it lies before our eyes. ...to the <term>Universal Work</term> <note>The ultimate alchemical goal: the creation of the Philosopher's Stone or the Universal Medicine.</note>, especially at the beginning of the work—should a <term>laboratory assistant</term> <note>original: "Laborant"; here referring to a practical worker or hireling.</note> demand such for his <term>process</term>, I advise every amateur <note>original: "Liebhaber"; a devotee or student of alchemy.</note> not to listen to such a man, but rather to flee from him. ...out, that without <term>Putrefaction</term> <note>original: "Putrefaction"; the process of decay or fermentation, which alchemists believed was a necessary stage of "death" that a substance must undergo before it could be "reborn" in a perfected state.</note> nothing perfect can result. From this, a <term>seeker</term> <note>original: "Liebhaber"; literally a "lover" of the art, referring to a student or amateur practitioner of alchemy.</note> can see how far one can go astray when one works so blindly in the world, squandering one's gold and silver. Is it any wonder then, if one thereafter consumes everything they own and ends up with a <note>original: "Bettelstab"; literally a "beggar's staff," a common German idiom for falling into bankruptcy or extreme poverty.</note> beggar's staff?

40. In these points above, I have several times spoken to seekers—for the explanation of the philosophical writings—mostly of a **Sulfur** and **Mercury** <note>original: "Mercurio"; these are the two traditional active principles of alchemy, representing the masculine/fiery and feminine/fluid aspects of matter.</note>. Yet at the same time, I also made mention of the three fundamental principles of all things (<note>original: "in specie"; specifically</note> of the mineral kingdom) as **Sulfur**, **Salt**, & **Liquid** <note>original: "Sulphure, Sale & Liquore"; these represent the *Tria Prima*—the three primary components of matter according to Paracelsian alchemy.</note>, which some call the **radical moisture** <note>original: "humidum radicale"; the fundamental, life-sustaining fluid believed to exist within all created things.</note>. Those who call it the **radical moisture** are indeed right, for such is the true **Mercury of the Philosophers** <note>original: "Mercurius Philosophorum"; this is not common quicksilver, but the universal essence or "spirit" that alchemists sought to extract and refine.</note>, which is found in all things that have been created. Therefore it is also said: Adam brought it with him out of Paradise (when, concerning the matter, the...

41. But I fear that if my most esteemed seeker <note>original: "Liebhaber"; literally "lover" of the art, a common term for a dedicated student of alchemy.</note> does not know how to obtain ash <note>original: "Asche"; in alchemy, the "ash" often refers to the calcined remains of a substance from which the salt is extracted.</note>, then he will also find this salt only with great difficulty. Here, diligent reading must discover this secret. Most people smash their heads apart over the Mercury, which I have nonetheless explained quite clearly. It is not the common, running quicksilver <note>original: "Quecksilber"</note> that we mean; the running Mercury requires a great artist to bind its feet and wings. Much is required to "kill" it <note>To "kill" mercury (mercurius mortificatus) means to "fix" it—transforming it from a liquid, volatile state into a solid, stable form that can withstand heat.</note>, so that it may no longer rise again in its body. He who accomplishes this knows what he needs to know. As a sincere warning, I wish to report what a famous author writes about the Philosophical Mercury <note>original: "Mercurio Philosophico"; the refined, spiritualized essence of mercury used in the creation of the Philosophers' Stone, as opposed to common quicksilver.</note>.

42. First <note>original: "Primo"</note>, it is to be found in all places. Second <note>original: "Secundo"</note>, it is a son of the Art <note>original: "filius artis"; a term for a dedicated practitioner who has "given birth" to a substance through alchemical labor.</note> born from two saline substances <note>original: "salinischen Substanzen"</note> (which come from a single root). Third <note>original: "Tertio"</note>, it takes on all the natures of the planets <note>In alchemy, the planets correspond to the seven metals; thus, this Mercury contains the potential of all metals.</note>. Fourth <note>original: "Quarto"</note>, in this <term>Mercury</term> the quality of Earth dominates the quality of water, and it is a dry and coagulated water of a salty nature <note>original: "qualitas Terræ qualitatem aquæ, et est sicca, et coagulata salsæ naturæ aqua"</note>. Fifth <note>original: "Quinto"</note>, it is hot and dry. Sixth <note>original: "Sexto"</note>, it becomes increasingly compact through cooking <note>original: "Kochung"; the process of applying steady heat to a sealed vessel.</note>. Seventh <note>original: "Septimo"</note>, it is converted into a watery spirit and a fixed earth through a subtle <term>distillation</term>. Eighth <note>original: "Octavo"</note>, this contains within itself the so often-mentioned red and white incombustible <term>sulfur</term> <note>Red and white represent the two stages of the "Stone": the white for silver-making and the red for gold-making.</note>. Ninth <note>original: "Nono"</note>, it has in its innermost part a red and white <term>salt</term>. Now a <term>son of the art</term> <note>original: "filius artis"</note> can judge from this whether common running <term>Mercury</term> <note>original: "ordinari lauffende Mercurius"; common liquid quicksilver.</note> possesses these properties or not; herewith, from the above description, one can learn to recognize the difference between the philosophical and the common running <term>Mercury</term> only all too clearly. However, for one who already has a good understanding, much has been said herewith.

<page-num>51</page-num>

->* ( Mercury ☿ ) *<-

43. This **Mercury** described here is now the one and only that the **Philosophers** <note>original: "Philosophi"; refers to the historical lineage of master alchemists.</note> use, and nothing else besides it; for one sees indeed that it is neither gold nor silver. Herewith, I wish finally to warn everyone once more to beware of those who say they cannot begin their work, or process, without gold and silver; one should beware even more of those who say they can make gold and offer their processes for a certain price. Both are fraudsters; whoever can make gold needs no human assistance; rather, he is the one who can help so many thousands, just as **Ripley** <note>Sir George Ripley (c. 1415–1490), a renowned English alchemist. Legend says he donated massive sums to the Knights of St. John.</note> for many years sent one hundred thousand pounds of gold annually to the Knights of **Rhodes** for the maintenance of the war against the Turks, and as **Raymond Lully** <note>Raimundus Lullius (c. 1232–1315); legendary accounts claim he was invited to England to transmute gold for King Edward (likely Edward III) to fund a crusade.</note> did for King **Edward** in England, for the support <note>original: "Soutenirung"</note> of the holy war. However, it is also to be considered that it sometimes happens that some seekers <note>original: "Liebhaber"; refers to dedicated enthusiasts or students of alchemy.</note> in their works are very often led astray by wicked people and are deprived of everything by which they support their work and find their comfortable livelihood.

I, as I promised, most worthy friend! shall reveal my opinion quite sincerely. I know that every artist <note>The writer uses "artist" in the historical sense of a practitioner of the "Hermetic Art" or alchemy.</note> is zealous for his art; not one of them can bear to have it maligned. They suffer no contradiction; everything must be admired and believed, even to the point of *admiring* <note>original: "admiriren".</note> a black beetle in a sealed glass as if it were a devil banished into the glass. The Berchtesgaden work <note>original: "Berchtoldsgadener-Arbeit"; a reference to the famous wood carvings and folk art from the Berchtesgaden region, which the author views as intricate but perhaps trivial or based on "foolish fancies" rather than true value.</note> is also praised, yet not for its inherent value, but for its foolish fancies. I regard the master *chemists* <term>chemists: original "Chymisten"; at this time, chemistry and alchemy were not yet fully distinct disciplines, and practitioners often used the terms interchangeably.</term> like lovers who take their *frog* <note>original Latin: "Ranam".</note> for a *Diana* <note>original Latin: "Dianam"; the Roman goddess of beauty and the hunt. The writer is using a classical metaphor: just as a man in love might mistake a common frog for a beautiful goddess, these alchemists are blinded by their own delusions.</note>. I will therefore also proceed *point by point* <note>original: "punctatim".</note> and answer every *point* of their most esteemed *author*, and indeed *to* <note>The sentence ends mid-thought with "ad," the Latin word for "to," which likely leads into "Ad primum" or "To the first point" on the following page.</note>

1. Regarding the first point. It is not without reason that **philosophical studies** <note>original: "studium Philosophicum"</note> are considered one of the most beautiful of human sciences; however, I shall **explain** <note>original: "expliciren"</note> myself as to how the chemical science <note>In this context, the author refers to alchemy.</note> is connected to them. World-wisdom (by which I mean **philosophy**) serves not only as delightful entertainment for mankind, it also benefits the state, as we have seen sufficient evidence of in **experimental physics** <note>original: "Physica experimentali"</note>. But what **connection** <note>original: "Connexion"</note> the alchemists’ so-called **Stone** <term>Stone: original "Lapis"; the Philosopher's Stone, the legendary substance used to transmute metals and grant long life.</term> has with this, anyone trained in **philosophical study** <note>original: "studio Philosophico"</note> will likely be unable to grasp. For all that one can attribute to this chemical science—according to their own philosophical books—is that it is merely a **purely speculative science** <note>original: "scientia pure speculativa"</note>, because it cannot **establish** <note>original: "statuiren"</note> any positive **reasons** <note>original: "rationes"</note>; its **major premise** <note>original: "major"; the first part of a logical argument.</note> makes the **minor premise** <note>original: "minorem"; the second part of a logical argument.</note> doubtful and the **conclusion** <note>original: "Consequenz"</note> incorrect. To make their cause appear grand, they begin to compare their work to the **Work of Creation** <note>original: "Schöpfungswerk"</note>, but it is well known how poorly this comparison holds up. I call it "mechanical" because the result teaches me nothing other than what I bring about through physical work, even when I previously intended something else entirely in my **speculations** <note>The author is contrasting theoretical "speculative" science with the "mechanical" reality of trial and error.</note>. <page-num>62</page-num>

...should have been able to make gold; they were great world-sages and students of nature <note>original: "Naturkundiger"</note> who recognized how one thing flows into another like a <term>perpetuum mobile: a machine or system in perpetual motion</term>, which sustains and destroys the world and everything within it. They strove to understand the virtues and effects of every substance, although in many cases they only reached such knowledge through long <term>praxim: practice or application</term> and the observation of unavoidable results. They never once thought of capturing the "spirit-mover" and "sustainer" of nature, nor of giving it a physical body <note>original: "incorporiren"</note> and making a medicine out of it to improve base metals. 

The interpreters of these writings—the Egyptians and the Greeks—were the first who, already famous for their mastery of the **magic art** <note>original: "Zauber-Kunst," here referring to esoteric or occult sciences</note> and having practiced it tirelessly, pored over the books of <term>Hermetis: Hermes Trismegistus, the legendary Hellenistic figure to whom many foundational alchemical and hermetic texts were attributed</term> and transformed them into something entirely different. One must not blindly copy from one author to the next; for when one reads the <term>originalia: the original sources or records</term> concerning the lives and histories of these sages in their mother tongues, one finds not a single word about making gold. These philosophers <note>original: "Philosophi"</note> were of a far more sublime way of thinking,

<meta>catchword: art,</meta>

<summary>The author argues that ancient sages were natural philosophers interested in the mechanics of the universe, and that the "gold-making" interpretation of their work was a later corruption by Egyptian and Greek occultists.</summary>

<keywords>natural philosophy, alchemy, Hermes Trismegistus, perpetual motion, Egyptians, Greeks, history of science, ancient sages</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the argument from page 62, where the author asserts that ancient sages were observers of nature's laws rather than practitioners of alchemy. The text specifically challenges the idea that famous figures like Paracelsus (Theophrastus) actually performed transmutation, pointing out logical inconsistencies in their writings.</meta>

<page-num>63</page-num>

<header>( ⁂ )</header>

...kind, than that they should have spent their time on the chemical or alchemical art, insofar as it speaks of the <term>tincture: a potent substance or "philosopher's stone" capable of transmuting base metals into gold or curing all diseases</term>. In their paganism, they sought the creator of this universe <note>original: "Universi"</note>, and this they must also have found, namely: that there is an author of nature <note>original: "Auctor naturæ"</note>. From this nature they attained a knowledge of his omnipotence, into which some delved more deeply and others less. 

According to the general state of their understanding, one must derive the correctness of these matters from a distant antiquity; and how uncertain this is can be found in the book *On the Nature of the Gods* <note>original: "de natura Deorum," a philosophical treatise by the Roman orator Cicero</note>. What uncertainty do we not also get from the more recent writers? Even in Salzburg itself, they want to know nothing of the claim that **Theophrastus** <note>Theophrastus Bombastus von Hohenheim, known as Paracelsus (1493–1541), a Swiss physician and alchemist who died in Salzburg</note> could make gold. He was a famous physician <note>original: "Medicus"</note>, and that in itself is enough to gather great treasures. 

How untruthfully does he speak against the other philosophers <note>original: "Philosophos"</note> who say that there is only one single thing—a thing that is found in all places? Why then did he have to fetch one ingredient from Hungary and the other from <term>Istria: a peninsula at the head of the Adriatic Sea, shared today by Italy, Slovenia, and Croatia</term>? These are indeed two different things; and if only those specific materials from Hungary and Istria are suitable for the work, then the "thing" is certainly not to be found everywhere.

Oh, would to God that every enthusiast would examine himself according to the sixth point—not seeking to manufacture the **Philosopher’s Stone** <term>Philosopher’s Stone: a legendary substance alchemists believed could turn base metals into gold and provide the "elixir of life"</term>, but rather to consider with sound reason whether he or another of his colleagues might be capable of truly learning the science from so many hundreds of old and new chemical books currently in existence. No, they say themselves it is impossible, and here they speak the truth; for the entire art consists only in the imagination of men. This imagination, darkened by the greed for money, has dragged many thousands into the most pitiable abyss. And who is to blame? None other than the **chemical authors** <note>the writers of alchemical treatises</note>. So long as they write of beloved **Nature**, how she works and labors in her workshop, what she brings forth, how her rising and falling, her killing and reviving, or her **perpetual motion** <note>original: "motus perpetuus"; in this context, the eternal cycles of birth, death, and transformation in the natural world</note> circulates—oh! until then they are pleasant and to be esteemed above all men. They enlighten and guide reason, and lead us at every occurrence—indeed, at every glance—back to her origin. Such **philosophers** <note>original: "Philosophos"</note> one must honor and utilize, because they make more intimately known to us that which

I say only that the male [principle] requires the female seed <term>Saamen</term> <note>In early modern science, "seed" referred to the generative essence of any substance, not just biological seeds.</note> for two reasons: one for the attainment, and the other for the taking up or receiving of the seed into itself. This is just as the earth does for the vegetable [kingdom] and the rocks do for the mineral [kingdom], with the difference that in the latter two, only the conception and mere preservation of the seed occurs, whereas in the former [the animal kingdom], in addition to these, the mixing [of seeds] takes place.

Some botanists <term>Botanici</term> <note>original: "Botanici"</note> do indeed wish to claim that there are male and female species <term>Species</term> <note>original: "Species"</note> among herbs and trees; but this is only to be understood in so far as some are of a drier, and others of a cooler and moister property. Otherwise, it would have to follow that there were also two genders in grass, oak, and birch trees. Furthermore, the distinction in the mineral kingdom—where one thing is made a man and the other a woman—is something we have to thank the astrologers <term>Astrologis</term> <note>original: "Astrologis"</note> and poets <term>Poeten</term> <note>original: "Poeten"; the author suggests that gendering minerals (like calling Gold "Sun/Male" and Silver "Moon/Female") is a literary or astrological invention rather than a biological reality.</note> for,

The **philosophers** <note>original: "Philosophi." In early modern texts, "Art" refers to human technology and alchemy, which seeks to imitate or perfect "Nature."</note> to achieve exactly what nature does in the earth—much less anything better—outside of its natural environment.

Furthermore, how could we replicate the **mother-place** <term>Mutterstätte</term> <note>The geological "womb" or matrix—the specific rock formations where metals were thought to be conceived and grown.</note> and other such requirements?

...and so forth; that lead contains silver, and copper contains gold, and that several ores are to be found in a single rock, is to be attributed to the proportioned seed. For, however much of the silver seed was distributed with the lead seed, that much also became silver. Here one sees quite clearly that the **perfection** <note>In alchemy, "perfection" refers to the purity and maturity of a metal, with gold being the most "perfect."</note> of these metals does not depend <note>original: *dependire*</note> on the cooking <note>The alchemical theory that subterranean heat "cooks" or matures base metals into noble ones over time.</note>; for otherwise, in the cold <term>Saturnine</term> <note>Associated with the planet Saturn; the alchemical name for lead-based minerals.</note> mineral, the silver would never have ripened. 

One might try to refute me through various experiments <note>original: *experimenta*</note> performed with <term>Saturn</term> <note>The alchemical name for lead.</note> and sulfur; however, I will not allow any ripening to be proven to me by this, for this is not the sulfur, say the philosophers <note>The "Philosophers" refers to the ancient and medieval alchemical authorities.</note>, which brings about ripening. It is true that everything grows in the earth, but it does not follow that each thing should not have its own seed. For where does one hear that when a silver vein is exhausted, copper should grow within it over time? I do not wish to dispute the kinship of these metals; regarding the point of ripening, I will only mention that I cannot possibly be persuaded that the difference between the metals—and even less the difference between the **Vegetable** and **Animal Kingdoms**—[is merely a matter of degree or heat...]

...from the heart of **Saturn** <note>original: "Saturni"; in alchemy, Saturn is the symbolic name for the metal Lead.</note> it shall be drawn and applied as the **material of the Stone** <note>original: "pro materia Lapidis"; the base substance used to create the Philosopher's Stone.</note>; how many authors, on the contrary, do not reject saltpetre <note>original: "Nitrum"</note>, alum, vitriol, common table salt, and as I have already mentioned, all **salts** <note>original: "Salia"</note>? Is this not something to wonder at in such a great man? Does this mean acting sincerely? When I wish to eat beef, which must be cooked for nourishment and taste <note>original: "Gusto"</note>, must I not first have it raw? And is the raw meat not precisely flesh? It is as if I also wanted to say: you must salt the food, but by no means use any salt. Are these not strange phrases? Can you, my reader, find your way through such **contradictions** <note>original: "Contradictoria"</note>? 

Regardless of this, others cannot be deterred and stick to their position: because the **Mercury of the Philosophers** <note>original: "Mercurius Philosophorum"; not common quicksilver, but the "secret" mercury believed to be the soul of all metals.</note> arises **from a double saline species** <note>original: "ex duplici specie salina"</note>, it must therefore be salt. <meta>This page continues the author’s critique of alchemical writers who claim to be the only ones telling the truth while accusing others of deception. It picks up from page 104, where the author advised the reader to compare different writers and look for commonalities.</meta>

<header>❦ ( ⁂ ) ❦</header>

...generally <note>original: "ge-"; likely completing a word like "gemein" (common) from the previous page, referring to points where authors commonly agree.</note> agree to tell the **seeker** <note>original: "Liebhaber"; literally "lover" of the art, referring to a devoted student of alchemy.</note> the truth, and to show the clear, pure, and infallible way, and to lead him upon it. Each one wants to be an honest man; each one assures the blind seeker most solemnly that no one has written as clearly as he has; indeed, that it is only the reader’s fault if he cannot understand it, and other such foolish nonsense. Can a person, then, who possesses even a little healthy **reason** <note>original: "Vernunft"</note>—and one not darkened by a **greed for gold** <note>original: "Goldbegierde"</note>—willfully deliver themselves into the hands of so many tyrants? ...were the ancient **writers**? Who is the guarantor of the truth that they actually possessed this **art** <note>The "art" refers to alchemy and the creation of the Philosopher's Stone.</note>? ...[material might] be worked into something far nobler and more perfect than Nature itself can produce. See, dearest friend, what **contradictions** <note>original: *Contradictoria*. If Nature, through her own effect and labor, brings forth the little herb **Moonwort** <note>original: *Lunariam*. I do not wish to refute this proposition, but only to answer this much: that the **quintessence** <note>original: *Quint-Essenz*. Literally the "fifth essence," the purest and most concentrated medicinal or spiritual core of a substance, extracted through alchemical processes.</note> is already hidden within this little herb, though it is not manifest. Regardless of this, no Art will ever succeed in reaching the point where one can take the quintessence of the little herb and, with and through that same quintessence, recreate the herb itself once again.

...can. I do not deny the existence of a **quintessence** <note>original: *Quint-Essenz*. The "fifth essence" or purest, most concentrated extract of a substance.</note>, but just as little as a small herb can grow back from it, so little do I believe that the **Gentlemen Alchemists** <note>original: *Alchymici*. Latin for alchemists.</note> are able—out of or by virtue of their quintessence—to bring black **lead** <note>original: *Bley*.</note> into white **silver** or **mercury** <note>original: *Mercurium*.</note> into **gold**. 

It is true that, if one looks at it correctly, quintessences are brought about through a **simple cooking** <note>original: *schlechte Kochung*. In early modern German, *schlecht* often meant "plain," "simple," or "direct" rather than "bad" or "poor quality."</note>. But as for what the **philosophers** <note>original: *Philosophi*. Referring to the historical authorities of alchemical theory.</note> mean by their "simple cooking," I would wager that many thousands of **enthusiasts** <note>original: *Liebhaber*. And just as little as they understand the actual word "cooking," so little do they know the right pot. 

I would almost wager that many of the **authors** <note>original: *Authores*.</note> who wrote about it do not understand it themselves. One also finds no evidence that the **Adepts** <note>original: *Adepti*. Those who have supposedly mastered the secret of the Great Work.</note> have explained themselves more precisely on the matter. The master **authors** <term>Authores</term> <note>Historical alchemical authorities whose texts were often intentionally obscure to protect their secrets from the "unworthy."</note> claimed they could not write more clearly without completely revealing the **secret** <note>original: *Geheimnuß*. A Latin idiom suggesting that a person of intellect requires no further explanation.</note>. On one hand, these writings are like roasted chickens upon which a whole swarm of bees falls with the greatest desire, beginning to gnaw at them from every side; on the other hand, it seems they used these words because they themselves did not know what they should write, in order to conclude their ending as confusedly as their beginning had been. By doing so, they sustained a book that contained nothing but an incomprehensible understanding. Each one proves that he says too much while actually saying nothing, and finally, they direct the seeker to the dear **gates of nature** <note>original: *Naturpforten*. A metaphorical term for the fundamental principles of the natural world that an alchemist must "enter" to succeed.</note> without giving him the right key to open them.

A Latin phrase describing a person who relies on humble, direct observation rather than the convoluted, often deceptive theories of the highly "learned."</note> than an over-educated scholar. ... [let] one torment them, just as they have tormented many an innocent **lover** <note>In this context, a "lover" (*Liebhaber*) refers to a "Lover of the Art"—a dedicated seeker or student of alchemy.</note>, when they brought him to the ruin of all his possessions with empty promises. If they possessed the Art of which they write so much (which I, however, cannot comprehend), they should have actively demonstrated it for the benefit of their poor neighbor, of which, however, no example can be shown; if they did not know how to perform it and in their hearts believed nothing of it themselves, then they should not have ruined so many people through their writings. Both kinds of these authors are at all times punishable. 

The former should rather have left their pens stuck in a **dead head** <term>Caput Mortuum</term> <note>original: *Caput Mortuum* (Latin); literally "dead head." In alchemy, this refers to the useless, sterile residue remaining in a vessel after all "spirit" or volatile matter has been distilled away.</note> and thrown it (as many have done) onto the dung heap, rather than wishing to bring their unrefined nature to light. They contradict themselves; they are duty-bound to preserve the **Secret** <term>Geheimnuß</term> <note>The "Secret" (*Geheimnuß*) refers to the "Great Work" or the hidden knowledge of the Philosopher's Stone, which was traditionally meant to be kept from the unworthy.</note>, to love God and their neighbor as themselves, yet they have accomplished none of these. What, then, should one think of such people? 

The latter kind of these people is damnable because of their **greed for money** <term>Geldbegierde</term>,

<summary>The author advocates for the harsh punishment of alchemical writers, arguing that they are either selfish hoarders of knowledge or, more likely, malicious frauds who bankrupt their followers. He invokes the principle of "lex talionis" to justify their symbolic and literal condemnation.</summary>

Since their **Philosopher's Stone** <term>Lapis</term> <note>original: *Lapis* (Latin); referring to the *Lapis Philosophorum* or the legendary substance capable of turning base metals into gold.</note> did not produce gold for them, they sought it through the ruin of their neighbors. From this you can see, my friend, what poisonous snakes are maintained within the state. Do not be angry with me, for sound reason and experience must convince you that I rightly and zealously speak out against those who blaspheme the Divine Scripture—who profane it and deny **Nature** its **power** <term>potentiam</term> <note>original: *potentiam* (Latin); referring to the inherent creative or transformative power within the natural world.</note>. 

You know that I too was once one of the **followers of Hermes** <note>original: *Hermetis clienten*; "clients" or disciples of Hermes Trismegistus, the mythical father of alchemy.</note> and worshiped his **A.B.C. Table** <note>The "A.B.C. Overlook such things, dearest friend, and believe me: as I barely attained some knowledge of **inscrutable Nature** and measured my undertakings against it, it seemed to me that I was nevertheless moving further and further from my ultimate goal. Because of this, the desire to produce the **Philosopher's Stone** <term>Stein der Weisen</term> diminished more and more, for I began to perceive the difficulty of the matter much more **vividly** <note>The text ends with the fragment "leb-", completed by the catchword "haf-" to form *lebhaft* (vividly/clearly).</note>...

<header>->⚜ ( A ) ⚜<-</header>

...to recognize more clearly, and to consider the wretched ends of all those who sought such things, along with the doubts that arose within me as to whether such a **Tincture** <term>Tinctur</term> <note>original: *Tinctur*; a potent substance or "elixir" sought by alchemists to transform base metals into gold or to create a universal medicine.</note> could even exist.

I could not possibly grasp how a **spirit** <term>Geist</term> <note>In alchemical terms, a "spirit" is the volatile, subtle essence extracted from a substance, often through distillation.</note> should take on a bodily form—a spirit that has been drawn out of a body—for it seemed to me that no spirit, except the **ethereal** <note>original: *ätherischen*; referring to the "ether" or the quintessential celestial element, which was believed to be the only force capable of bridging the gap between spirit and matter.</note> one, could possess such power and capability. It annoyed me that the **philosophers** <note>original: *Philosophi*; referring here to the authors of alchemical treatises who considered themselves "Natural Philosophers."</note> make such presumptuous allegories regarding this **embodiment of the spirit** <term>Spiritus Corporification</term> <note>original: *Spiritus Corporification* (Latin/German hybrid); the theoretical process of fixing a volatile spirit into a stable, solid material state.</note> by comparing it with the **Incarnation of Christ** <note>original: *Menschwerdung Christi*; the central Christian doctrine of God becoming human, which the author believes is being profaned by these chemical comparisons.</note>. In that context, one speaks of **light from light** <note>original: *lumen de lumine* (Latin); a phrase from the Nicene Creed used to describe the relationship between God the Father and God the Son.</note> and a spirit from the spirit, and not a spirit out of a bodily matter. 

All their **allegories** and parables are of this nature; one must forcibly twist them toward their intended meaning, and they are inexhaustible wells of all confusion. All sovereigns and republics should eradicate such books that profane the **Holy Scripture**.

Dearest friend! I imagine that you, as well as all **seekers** <note>original: *Liebhaber*; literally "lovers" of the alchemical art.</note>, will be indignant with me. Perhaps there will be...

<summary>The author describes his mounting intellectual and religious objections to alchemy, specifically criticizing the "blasphemous" comparison of chemical processes to the Incarnation of Christ. ...there may be some who think: let us let the fool speak, our **experiments** <term>Experimenta</term> <note>original: *Experimenta*; practical chemical trials as opposed to pure theory.</note> convince us otherwise. A stubborn lover <note>original: *Liebhaber*; a "lover of the art," meaning an amateur or aspiring alchemist.</note> will perhaps bring forth many **arguments regarding the probability of the Stone** <note>original: *rationes de probabilitate Lapidis*; logical and scholastic proofs used to argue that the Philosopher's Stone is scientifically possible.</note> borrowed from the **authors** <term>Authoribus</term>—propositions that are learned and pleasant to hear. Venus might weaken me through her burning glances, Mercury’s two-headed serpent-staff <note>The Caduceus, a symbol of the god Mercury and often associated with the metal mercury in alchemy.</note> should gnaw at me, the poisonous **matter** <term>Materia</term> itself should suffocate me with its mist and steam, and I should never be allowed to see the **Sun and Moon** <note>In alchemy, the Sun (Sol) and Moon (Luna) represent gold and silver, respectively.</note> for all eternity—such is how I imagine the wrath of the **chemical writers**; but since I wear the **Panacea** <note>The "Panacea" was a mythical universal cure-all sought by alchemists.</note> of truth upon my breast, I fear nothing.

Therefore, believe me, my friend, I am more sincere than all your writers; I will tell you clearly what the true **Philosopher's Stone** <note>original: *Lapis Philosophorum*; the legendary substance capable of turning base metals into gold and granting eternal life.</note> is. It is **God**; in His triangle of the Trinity you find the **Sulfur** <note>original: *Sulphur*; the Paracelsian principle of soul and combustibility.</note> of His unquenchable fire, the **Mercury** <note>original: *Mercurium*; the Paracelsian principle of spirit and fluidity.</note> of His overflowing emanations, and the **Salt** <note>original: *Salz*; the Paracelsian principle of body and stability.</note> of His everlasting constancy, through which everything created is preserved. You find all three properties in one; this is the true triangle of the most high Trinity, and yet of one Godhead.
